Differential symmetry breaking operators from a line bundle to a vector bundle over real projective spaces
Toshihisa Kubo

Abstract
. In this paper we classify and construct differential symmetry breaking operators 𝔻 \mathbb{D} from a line bundle over the real projective space ℝℙ n \mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n} to a vector bundle over ℝℙ n − 1 \mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1} . We further determine the factorization identities of 𝔻 \mathbb{D} and the branching laws of the corresponding generalized Verma modules of 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ​ ( n + 1 , ℂ ) \mathfrak{sl}(n+1,\mathbb{C}) . By utilizing the factorization identities, the S ​ L ​ ( n , ℝ ) SL(n,\mathbb{R}) -representations realized on the image Im ⁡ ( 𝔻 ) \mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D}) are also investigated.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
22E46, 17B10

1.
Introduction

Intertwining operators are some of the fundamental objects in representation theory. For instance, Knapp–Stein operators play a key role in the representation theory of real reductive groups. In this paper, we consider the classification and
construction of
certain intertwining differential operators called

differential symmetry breaking operators
.
In order to describe the main problems of this paper,
we start the introduction with the definition of such operators.

1.1.
Differential symmetry breaking operators and main problems

Let
X
be a smooth manifold and
Y
a smooth submanifold of
X
.
Take
G^{\prime}\subset G
to be a pair of Lie groups that act transitively
on
Y
and
X
, respectively.
Suppose that

\mathcal{V}\to X
and
\mathcal{W}\to Y
are

G
- and
G^{\prime}
-equivariant
vector bundles over
X
and
Y
with fibers
V
and
W
, respectively.
Then a continuous linear operator

\mathbb{A}\colon C^{\infty}(X,\mathcal{V})\to C^{\infty}(Y,\mathcal{W})
between
the spaces of smooth sections is called
a
symmetry breaking operator

if
\mathbb{A}
is
G^{\prime}
-intertwining
[
18
]
.
Hereafter, we shall often abbreviate it as an SBO.

Suppose that there exists an inclusion

\iota\colon Y\hookrightarrow X
. In this case, although the base manifolds

X
and
Y
are different, one can define a differential operator

\mathbb{D}\colon C^{\infty}(X,\mathcal{V})\to C^{\infty}(Y,\mathcal{W})

with respect to the inclusion
\iota

(see
[
15
]
for the details).
We call the differential operator
\mathbb{D}

a
differential symmetry breaking operator

if
\mathbb{D}
is also an SBO (cf.
[
12
,
13
,
15
,
17
]
).
The systematic study of SBOs
\mathbb{A}
and differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}

have been initiated by
T. Kobayashi with his collaborators over a decade
(cf.
[
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
19
]
).

There are three main problems in this paper. To describe them in a general framework, let
G
be a real reductive Lie group and
G^{\prime}\subset G
a reductive subgroup of
G
.
Let
P=MAN_{+}
and
P^{\prime}=M^{\prime}A^{\prime}N_{+}^{\prime}
be Langlands decompositions
of
P
and
P^{\prime}
, respectively, with
M^{\prime}A^{\prime}\subset MA
and
N_{+}^{\prime}\subset N_{+}
.
We denote by
\mathrm{Irr}(M)_{\mathrm{fin}}
and
\mathrm{Irr}(M^{\prime})_{\mathrm{fin}}
the sets of equivalence classes of
finite-dimensional irreducible representations of
M
and
M^{\prime}
, respectively.
Likewise, we write
\mathrm{Irr}(A)
and
\mathrm{Irr}(A^{\prime})
for the sets of characters of
A
and

A^{\prime}
, respectively. Then, for the outer tensor products

\xi\boxtimes\lambda\boxtimes\mathrm{triv}

of
(\xi,\lambda)\in\mathrm{Irr}(M)_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathrm{Irr}(A)
and

\varpi\boxtimes\nu\boxtimes\mathrm{triv}

of

(\varpi,\nu)\in\mathrm{Irr}(M^{\prime})_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathrm{Irr}(A^{\prime})
with
the trivial representations
\mathrm{triv}
of
N_{+}
and
N_{+}^{\prime}
,
we put

I(\xi,\lambda)=\mathrm{Ind}_{P}^{G}(\xi\boxtimes\lambda\boxtimes\mathrm{triv})\quad\text{and}\quad J(\varpi,\nu)=\mathrm{Ind}_{P^{\prime}}^{G^{\prime}}(\varpi\boxtimes\nu\boxtimes\mathrm{triv})

for (unnormalized) parabolically induced representations of
G
and
G^{\prime}
,
respectively.
We denote by

\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\xi,\lambda),J(\varpi,\nu))
the space of differential SBOs

\mathbb{D}\colon I(\xi,\lambda)\to J(\varpi,\nu)
.

1.2.
Classification and construction of
\mathbb{D}

The first problem concerns the classification and construction of differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}
.
More precisely, we consider the following problem.

Problem A

(Classification and construction of
\mathbb{D}
)
.

Do the following.

(A1)

Classify
(\xi,\lambda,\varpi,\nu)
such that

\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\xi,\lambda),J(\varpi,\nu))\neq\{0\}.

(A2)

Determine

\dim\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\xi,\lambda),J(\varpi,\nu)).

(A3)

Construct generators

\mathbb{D}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\xi,\lambda),J(\varpi,\nu)).

1.3.
Factorization identity of
\mathbb{D}

The second problem concerns a decomposition formula of
\mathbb{D}
.
Observe that the composition
\mathcal{A}_{J}\circ\mathbb{A}_{1}
of a
(not necessarily differential) SBO

\mathbb{A}_{1}\colon I(\xi_{1},\lambda_{1})\to J(\varpi_{1},\nu_{1})
with
a
G^{\prime}
-intertwining operator

\mathcal{A}_{J}\colon J(\varpi_{1},\nu_{1})\to J(\varpi_{2},\nu_{2})
is an SBO

\mathcal{A}_{J}\circ\mathbb{A}_{1}\colon I(\xi_{1},\lambda_{1})\to J(\varpi_{2},\nu_{2}).

In a diagram we have

0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{J(\varpi_{2},\nu_{2})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Likewise, the composition
\mathbb{A}_{2}\circ\mathcal{A}_{I}

of a
G
-intertwining operator

\mathcal{A}_{I}\colon I(\xi_{1},\lambda_{1})\to I(\xi_{2},\lambda_{2})
with
an SBO
\mathbb{A}_{2}\colon I(\xi_{2},\lambda_{2})\to J(\varpi_{2},\nu_{2})

is also an SBO

\mathbb{A}_{2}\circ\mathcal{A}_{I}\colon I(\xi_{1},\lambda)\to J(\varpi_{2},\nu_{2}),

that is,

For a given SBO

\mathbb{A}\colon I(\xi_{1},\lambda_{1})\to J(\varpi_{2},\nu_{2})
,
we call the identities

\mathbb{A}=\mathcal{A}_{J}\circ\mathbb{A}_{1}=\mathbb{A}_{2}\circ\mathcal{A}_{I}

the
factorization identities
of
\mathbb{A}
.
We remark that such an identity is also known as
a
functional equation
in the literature (cf.
[
18
,
19
]
).
The diagram (
1.3
) below illustrates the identities.

15.52084pt\raise-17.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.70752pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circlearrowleft}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 34.29169pt\raise-35.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 53.29169pt\raise-35.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{J(\varpi_{2},\nu_{2})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

In this paper we consider the factorization identities for differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}
.

Problem B

(Factorization identities of
\mathbb{D}
)
.

Compute the factorization identities

\mathbb{D}=\mathcal{D}_{J}\circ\mathbb{D}_{1}=\mathbb{D}_{2}\circ\mathcal{D}_{I}

(1.1)

of a differential SBO

\mathbb{D}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\xi,\lambda),J(\varpi,\nu))
.

For preceding works on the factorization identities, see, for instance,
Fischmann–Juhl–Somberg
[
2
]
,
Juhl
[
10
]
,
Kobayashi–Kubo–Pevzner
[
14
]
,
Kobayashi–Pevzner
[
16
]
, and
Kobayashi–Ørsted–Somberg–Souček
[
17
]
,
for differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}
.
For (not necessarily differential) SBOs
\mathbb{A}
, see, for instance,
Hader
[
5
]
and
Kobayashi–Speh
[
18
,
19
]
.
We remark that although (
1.1
) expresses

\mathbb{D}
in two ways, namely,
\mathbb{D}=\mathcal{D}_{J}\circ\mathbb{D}_{1}
and

\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}_{2}\circ\mathcal{D}_{I}
, it seems more natural for
\mathbb{D}
(or
\mathbb{A}
)
to satisfy only one of the
identities. We consider the “double factorization identities”

\mathbb{D}=\mathcal{D}_{J}\circ\mathbb{D}_{1}=\mathbb{D}_{2}\circ\mathcal{D}_{I}
in this paper.

1.4.
The image
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D})

As an SBO
\mathbb{A}\colon I(\xi,\lambda)\to J(\varpi,\nu)

is
G^{\prime}
-intertwining, the image
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{A})
is naturally a
G^{\prime}
-invariant subspace
of
J(\varpi,\nu)
. In fact, in the fundamental work of Kobayashi–Speh

[
18
,
19
]
over SBOs
\mathbb{A}
for the pair

(G,G^{\prime})=(O(n+1,1),O(n,1))
, they classified
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{A})
at the level of

(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},K^{\prime})
-modules, among many other things.
In this paper, we also aim to determine
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D})
for differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}
.

Problem C

(Determination of
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D})
)
.

Determine
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D})
of

\mathbb{D}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\xi,\lambda),J(\varpi,\nu))
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{D}
satisfies a factorization identity

\mathbb{D}=\mathcal{D}_{J}\circ\mathbb{D}_{1}=\mathbb{D}_{2}\circ\mathcal{D}_{I}

as in (
1.1
). Then

\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{D}_{I})
is a
G^{\prime}
-subrepresentation of
I(\xi_{1},\lambda_{1})
,
and
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{D}_{J})
and
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
are
both
G^{\prime}
-invariant subspaces of
J(\varpi_{1},\nu_{1})
, that is, we have

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.89732pt\raise-38.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I(\xi_{2},\lambda_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.89731pt\raise-62.6611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.91112pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{D}_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.29169pt\raise-56.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.52084pt\raise-38.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.70752pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circlearrowleft}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 34.29169pt\raise-56.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 53.29169pt\raise-56.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{J(\varpi_{2},\nu_{2})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(1.2)

In this paper we also investigate
a relationship between
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{D}_{J})
and
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
.
Further, observe that as

(\mathcal{D}_{J}\circ\mathbb{D}_{1})|_{\text{Ker}(\mathcal{D}_{I})}=(\mathbb{D}_{2}\circ\mathcal{D}_{I})|_{\text{Ker}(\mathcal{D}_{I})}=0,

we have
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D}_{1}|_{\text{Ker}(\mathcal{D}_{I})})\subset\text{Ker}(\mathcal{D}_{J})
.
We then consider whether or not the equality

\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D}_{1}|_{\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{D}_{I})})=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{D}_{J})
holds.
This is somewhat analogous to the work
[
5
]
of
Hader for his study of the Heisenberg wave operator in sprit.

1.5.
SL
vs
GL

The aim of this paper is to answer Problems
A
,

B
, and
C
for differential SBOs

\mathbb{D}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\xi,\lambda),J(\varpi,\nu))

for the case

(G/P,G^{\prime}/P^{\prime})\simeq(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
,
the real projective spaces of dimension

n
and
n-1
, respectively, with

\xi\in\mathrm{Irr}(M)_{\mathrm{fin}}
for
\dim\xi=1
.
We allow the inducing representation
\varpi
for
J(\varpi,\nu)

to be any
\varpi\in\mathrm{Irr}(M^{\prime})_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
For the purpose, there are at least two choices on
(G,G^{\prime})
, namely,

(G,G^{\prime})=(SL(n+1,\mathbb{R}),SL(n,\mathbb{R}))
or
(GL(n+1,\mathbb{R}),GL(n,\mathbb{R}))
.
In this paper, we consider both cases for Problems
A
and
B
.
Only the
SL
-case is considered for Problem
C
.
We utilize our preceding results on
SL(n,\mathbb{R})
-intertwining differential operators
\mathcal{D}

to consider Problem
C
for the
SL
-case.

For Problem
A
, there is a significant difference between
the
GL
-case and
SL
-case for
n=2
. In the
GL
-case, it turned out that the space

\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\xi,\lambda),J(\varpi,\nu))
of differential SBOs for
\xi
with
\dim\xi=1

is multiplicity-free for all
n\geq 2
. In contrast, the dimension could be

\dim\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\xi,\lambda),J(\varpi,\nu))=2
in the
SL
-case for
n=2
.
This difference arises because there are more parameters in the
GL
-case
than the
SL
-case. For the details of the results, see Theorems
4.15

and
4.16
for the
SL
-case and Theorem
10.3
for the

GL
-case.

1.6.
Classification of SBOs
\mathbb{A}
between line bundles
over
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}

All symmetry breaking operators
\mathbb{A}\colon I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)\to J(\mathrm{triv},\nu)

including non-local operators
for
(G,G^{\prime})=(GL(n+1,\mathbb{R}),GL(n,\mathbb{R}))

with
\xi=\mathrm{triv}
and
\varpi=\mathrm{triv}
,
the trivial representations of
M
and
M^{\prime}
, respectively,
are already classified by Frahm–Weiske in
[
4
]
.
At first glance,
their classification seems to miss a family of differential SBOs

\mathbb{D}_{(m,\ell)}\colon I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)\to J(\mathrm{triv},\nu)
for
n=2

in Theorem
10.3
.
However, the differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}_{(m,\ell)}
can be thought of as
the residue operators of SBOs
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda,\nu}
of

[
4
, Thm. 3.12]
.
Therefore, their classification indeed includes
\mathbb{D}_{(m,\ell)}
implicitly.

It seems that one needs to be careful with the comments at the end of Section 1.3 of
[
4
]
on Knapp–Stein operators (standard intertwining operators).
The authors of the cited paper briefly commented that
there do not exist non-trivial Knapp–Stein operators for
the parabolically induced representations for
P^{\prime}\subset G^{\prime}
.
Nevertheless, if
n=2
, then
G^{\prime}=GL(2,\mathbb{R})
and
P^{\prime}
is a minimal parabolic
subgroup of
G^{\prime}
. Thus, non-trivial Knapp–Stein operators for
P^{\prime}\subset G^{\prime}
do
exist. Indeed, the aforementioned differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}_{(m,\ell)}

satisfy a factorization identity with a normal derivative and the residue operator
of a Knapp–Stein operator. We shall discuss some details in Remark
10.19
.

1.7.
F-method

The main machinery for us to classify differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}

on Problem
A
is the
F-method (cf.
[
2
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
).
Via the so-called
algebraic Fourier transform of generalized Verma modules
,
this method allows one to classify and construct differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}
simultaneously,
by solving a certain system of partial differential equations.
For the recent study of the F-method, see, for instance,
[
3
,
20
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
]
and the references therein.

1.8.
Branching law of generalized Verma modlues

Via the duality between differential SBOs and
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-homomorphisms
between generalized Verma modules (Theorem
2.3
),
the classification of differential SBOs is closely related to the branching law
of a generalized Verma module
M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda)
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The character identity in the Grothendieck group of the BGG category
\mathcal{O}^{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}

for a parabolic subalgebra
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime}\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}

yields the branching law of
M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda)
for generic parameter
\lambda
.
The branching law for singular
\lambda
requires
some information of the structures
of
M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda)
such as the classification of
{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}
-invariant subspaces of

M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda)
. See, for instance,
[
11
,
17
]

for the study of the branching laws of generalized Verma modules.

In this paper, in addition to the three main problems,
we also discuss the branching laws of generalized Verma modules
in consideration. For singular parameters, the results from the F-method play a key role.
We further present the branching law of the image of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
-homomorphisms as well.
These are accomplished in Theorems
9.22
and
9.27
.
Via the aforementioned duality, the resulting branching laws support our classification of differential SBOs.

1.9.
Organization of the paper

Now we describe the rest of the paper.
There are ten sections including the introduction.
The aim of Section
2
is to
give a quick overview of the F-method in a general
framework. At the end of the section, a recipe of the F-method will be presented,
which plays a central role for the classification and construction of differential
SBOs
\mathbb{D}
in this paper.
In Section
3
, we then specialize the framework to the case

(G,G^{\prime})=(SL(n+1,\mathbb{R}),SL(n,\mathbb{R}))
with maximal parabolic subgroups

P\subset G
and
P^{\prime}\subset G^{\prime}
such that
G/P\simeq\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n}
and

G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. Some necessary notation is introduced in this section.

The objective of Section
4
is to summarize the main results
of the classification and construction of differential SBOs

\mathbb{D}
(Problem
A
). Via the duality theorem (Theorem
2.3
),
we also discuss
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
- and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
-homomorphisms
\Phi
between
certain generalized Verma modules.
The proofs of the theorems in
Section
4
are discussed in
Sections
5
and
6
.
As mentioned above, for the
SL
-case,
the multiplicity-two phenomenon appears for
n=2
. Thus, we separate the proofs
into two cases, namely, the cases for
n\geq 3
and
n=2
. Section
5

deals with the former case; we handle the latter case in Section
6
.
In both cases, we follow the recipe of the F-method.

Section
7
is devoted to the factorization identities of
differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}
constructed in Section
4
.
We first give such identities for

({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-homomorphisms
\Phi
. We then convert them to ones for differential SBOs

\mathbb{D}
via the duality theorem. The factorization identities of
\mathbb{D}
are obtained in
Theorem
7.18
.
Some relevant results on intertwining differential operators and

({\mathfrak{g}},P)
- and
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-homomorphisms are also recalled from
[
20
]
in this section.

The images
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{D})
are determined in Section
8

for
\mathbb{D}
that satisfies the factorization identities (Problem
C
).
In this section we make use of some results
from
[
20
]
to determine them.
These are achieved in Section
8.2
.

The aim of Section
9
is to discuss
the branching laws of generalized Verma modules in consideration.
In this section, we first recall from
[
11
]
a character identity
of a generalized Verma module in a general framework to give
branching laws in the Grothendieck group of the BGG category
\mathcal{O}^{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}
.
We then apply
the character identity to the case
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})=(\mathfrak{sl}(n+1,\mathbb{C}),\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C}))

with maximal parabolic subalgebras
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}

considered above.
After discussing the decomposition of formal characters,
we give actual branching laws by utilizing the results from the F-method.
In this section, we also discucss the branching
law of the image of a
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
-homomorphism
from a generalized Verma module for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
to the one for
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
This supports the aforementioned multiplicity-two phenomenon
of
SL(n,\mathbb{R})
-differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}
for
n=2
.
The explicit branching laws are given in
Theorems
9.22
and
9.27
.

The last section, namely, Section
10
, is for the
GL
-counterpart of the results
of Problems
A
and
B
. In this section we give the classification of

GL(n,\mathbb{R})
-differential SBOs
\mathbb{D}
as well as their factorization identities. In principle,
the
GL(n,\mathbb{R})
-operators
\mathbb{D}
are the same as
the
SL(n,\mathbb{R})
-operators;
what one wishes to do is to classify the appropriate parameters. The main results are obtained in Theorems
10.3
and
10.7
.

2.
Preliminaries: the F-method

The aim of this section is to recall the so-called F-method.
In particular, we present a recipe of the F-method in Section
2.7
.
In Sections
5
and
6
, we shall follow the recipe to
classify and construct differential symmetry breaking operators in concern.
For the details of the F-method,
consult, for instance,
[
15
]
and
[
20
]
.
In this section we mainly take the expositions from
[
20
]
.

2.1.
Notation

Let
G
be a real reductive Lie group and
P=MAN_{+}
a Langlands decomposition of a parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
. We denote by
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})
and

{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{R})={\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
the Lie algebras of
G
and

P=MAN_{+}
, respectively.

For a real Lie algebra
\mathfrak{y}(\mathbb{R})
, we write
\mathfrak{y}

and
\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{y}})
for its complexification and the universal enveloping algebra of

{\mathfrak{y}}
, respectively.
For instance,
{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathfrak{a}}
, and
{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}
are the complexifications of
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R}),{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{R}),{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbb{R}),{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbb{R})
, and
{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
,
respectively.

For
\lambda\in{\mathfrak{a}}^{*}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbb{R}),\mathbb{C})
,
we denote by
\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}

the one-dimensional representation of
A
defined by

a\mapsto a^{\lambda}:=e^{\lambda(\log a)}
.
For a finite-dimensional irreducible
representation
(\sigma,V)
of
M
and
\lambda\in{\mathfrak{a}}^{*}
,
we denote by
\sigma_{\lambda}
the outer tensor product representation
\sigma\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}

on
V
,
namely,

\sigma_{\lambda}\colon ma\mapsto a^{\lambda}\sigma(m)
. By letting
N_{+}
act trivially, we regard

\sigma_{\lambda}
as a representation of
P
. Let
\mathcal{V}:=G\times_{P}V\to G/P

be the
G
-equivariant vector bundle over the real flag variety
G/P

associated with the
representation
(\sigma_{\lambda},V)
of
P
. We identify the Fréchet space

C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})
of smooth sections with

C^{\infty}(G,V)^{P}:=\{f\in C^{\infty}(G,V):f(gp)=\sigma_{\lambda}^{-1}(p)f(g)\;\;\text{for any $p\in P$}\},

the space of
P
-invariant smooth functions on
G
.
Then, via the left regular representation
L
of
G
on
C^{\infty}(G)
,
we realize the parabolically induced representation

\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)}=\mathrm{Ind}_{P}^{G}(\sigma_{\lambda})
on
C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})
.
We denote by
R
the right regular representation of
G
on
C^{\infty}(G)
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be a reductive subgroup of
G
and
P^{\prime}=M^{\prime}A^{\prime}N_{+}^{\prime}

a parabolic subgroup of
G^{\prime}

with
P^{\prime}\subset P

so that there exists a natural morphism
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}\to G/P

of the real flag variety
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}
to
G/P
.
We further assume that
M^{\prime}A^{\prime}\subset MA
and
N_{+}^{\prime}\subset N_{+}
.

As for
G/P
, for a finite-dimensional irreducible
representation
(\varpi_{\nu},W)
of
M^{\prime}A^{\prime}
, we define
an induced representation
\pi^{\prime}_{(\varpi,\nu)}=\mathrm{Ind}_{P^{\prime}}^{G^{\prime}}(\varpi_{\nu})
on the
space
C^{\infty}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})
of smooth sections for a

G^{\prime}
-equivariant vector bundle
\mathcal{W}:=G^{\prime}\times_{P^{\prime}}W\to G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}
.

Via the morphism
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}\to G/P
, one can define
differential operators
\mathbb{D}\colon C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})\to C^{\infty}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})

although
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}
and
G/P
are different manifolds
(see, for instance,
[
15
, Def. 2.1]
).
As
C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})
is a
G
-representation and
G^{\prime}\subset G
,
the space
C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})
is also a
G^{\prime}
-representation.
We then write
\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
for the space of

differential symmetry breaking operators

(
G^{\prime}
-intertwining differential operators)

\mathbb{D}\colon C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})\to C^{\infty}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})
.

Let

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{R})=\mathfrak{n}_{-}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{m}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{a}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{+}(\mathbb{R})

be the
Gelfand–Naimark decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{R})
,
and write
N_{-}=\exp({\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R}))
. We identify
N_{-}
with the
open Bruhat cell
N_{-}P
of
G/P
via the embedding

\iota\colon N_{-}\hookrightarrow G/P
,
\bar{n}\mapsto\bar{n}P
.
Via the restriction of the vector bundle
\mathcal{V}\to G/P
to the open Bruhat cell

N_{-}\mathrel{{\mathop{\hookrightarrow}\limits}}G/P
,
we regard
C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})
as a subspace of

C^{\infty}(N_{-})\otimes V
.

Likewise,
let

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})=\mathfrak{n}_{-}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{+}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})

be a
Gelfand–Naimark decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
,
and write
N_{-}^{\prime}=\exp({\mathfrak{n}}_{-}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
.
As for
C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})
, we regard
C^{\infty}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})

as a subspace of
C^{\infty}(N_{-}^{\prime})\otimes W
.

We often view differential symmetry breaking operators

\mathbb{D}\colon C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})\to C^{\infty}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})

as differential operators

\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}\colon C^{\infty}(N_{-})\otimes V\to C^{\infty}(N_{-}^{\prime})\otimes W

such that
the restriction
\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}|_{C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})}

to
C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})
is a map

\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}|_{C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})}\colon C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})\to C^{\infty}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})
(see (
2.1
) below).

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 34.97525pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.35483pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 58.97525pt\raise-32.01782pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\;\;C^{\infty}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.58943pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.58943pt\raise-16.00891pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.67209pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.58943pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

(2.1)

In particular, we often regard
\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
as

\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})\subset\mathrm{Diff}_{\mathbb{C}}(C^{\infty}(N_{-})\otimes V,C^{\infty}(N_{-}^{\prime})\otimes W).

(2.2)

2.2.
Duality theorem

For a finite-dimensional irreducible representation
(\sigma_{\lambda},V)
of
MA
,
we write
V^{\vee}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(V,\mathbb{C})
and
((\sigma_{\lambda})^{\vee},V^{\vee})
for
the contragredient representation of
(\sigma_{\lambda},V)
. By letting
{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}
act
on
V^{\vee}
trivially, we regard the infinitesimal representation

d\sigma^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{-\lambda}
of
(\sigma_{\lambda})^{\vee}
as a
{\mathfrak{p}}
-module. For a finite-dimensional irreducible representation

(\varpi_{\nu},W)
of
M^{\prime}A^{\prime}
, a
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
-module

d\varpi^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{-\nu}
is defined similarly.
We write

M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee})=\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{p}})}V^{\vee}\quad\text{and}\quad M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(W^{\vee})=\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime})}W^{\vee}

for generalized Verma modules for
({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{p}})
and
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime})
induced from

d\sigma^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{-\lambda}
and
d\varpi^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{-\nu}
,
respectively.
Via the diagonal action of
P
on
M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee})
, we regard

M_{\mathfrak{p}}(V^{\vee})
as a
({\mathfrak{g}},P)
-module. Likewise, we regard

M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(W^{\vee})
as a
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-module.

The following theorem is often called the
duality theorem
.
For the proof, see
[
15
]
.

Theorem 2.3

(Duality theorem)
.

There is a natural linear isomorphism

\mathcal{D}_{H\to D}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{P^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee}))\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\operatorname{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}).

(2.4)

Equivalently,

\mathcal{D}_{H\to D}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(W^{\vee}),M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee}))\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\operatorname{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}).

(2.5)

Here,
for
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{P^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee}))
and

F\in C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})\simeq C^{\infty}(G,V)^{P}
,
the section
\mathcal{D}_{H\to D}(\varphi)F\in C^{\infty}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})\simeq C^{\infty}(G^{\prime},W)^{P^{\prime}}

is given by

\langle\mathcal{D}_{H\to D}(\varphi)F,w^{\vee}\rangle=\sum_{j}\langle dR(u_{j})F,v_{j}^{\vee}\rangle\big|_{G^{\prime}}\;\;\text{for $w^{\vee}\in W^{\vee}$},

(2.6)

where
\varphi(w^{\vee})=\sum_{j}u_{j}\otimes v_{j}^{\vee}\in M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee})
.

2.3.
Algebraic Fourier transform
\widehat{\;\;\cdot\;\;}
of Weyl algebras

Let
U
be a complex finite-dimensional vector space with
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}U=n
.
Fix a basis
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}
of
U
and let

(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
denote the coordinates of
U
with respect to the basis.
Then the algebra

\mathbb{C}[U;z,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}]:=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z_{1}},\ldots,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z_{n}}]

with relations
z_{i}z_{j}=z_{j}z_{i}
,

\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}
,
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}z_{i}=\delta_{i,j}+z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}

is called the Weyl algebra of
U
, where
\delta_{i,j}
is the Kronecker delta.
Similarly,
let
(\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n})
denote the coordinates of
the dual space
U^{\vee}
of
U
with respect to the dual basis of

u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}
. We write

\mathbb{C}[U^{\vee};\zeta,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\zeta}]

for the Weyl algebra of
U^{\vee}
.
Then the map determined by

\widehat{\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}}:=-\zeta_{i},\quad\widehat{z_{i}}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial\zeta_{i}}

(2.7)

gives a Weyl algebra isomorphism

\widehat{\;\;\cdot\;\;}\;\colon\mathbb{C}[U;z,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}]\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathbb{C}[U^{\vee};\zeta,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\zeta}],\quad T\mapsto\widehat{T}.

(2.8)

The map (
2.8
) is called
the
algebraic Fourier transform of Weyl algebras

(
[
15
, Def. 3.1]
). We remark that the minus sign for “
-\zeta_{i}
”
in (
2.7
) is put in such a way that the resulting map
\widehat{\;\;\cdot\;\;}

is indeed a Weyl algebra homomorphism.

2.4.
Fourier transformed representation
\widehat{d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}}

For a representation
\eta
of
G
, we denote by
d\eta

the infinitesimal representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})
.
For instance,
dL
and
dR
denote the infinitesimal representations
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})
of
the left and right regular representations of
G
on
C^{\infty}(G)
.
As usual, we naturally extend representations of
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})

to ones for its universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
of its complexification
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The same convention is applied for closed subgroups of
G
.

For
g\in N_{-}MAN_{+}
, we write

g=p_{-}(g)p_{0}(g)p_{+}(g),

where
p_{\pm}(g)\in N_{\pm}
and
p_{0}(g)\in MA
.
Similarly, for
Y\in{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{n}}_{-}\oplus{\mathfrak{l}}\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}
with
{\mathfrak{l}}={\mathfrak{m}}\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}
,
we write

Y=Y_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}}+Y_{{\mathfrak{l}}}+Y_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}},

where
Y_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{\pm}}\in{\mathfrak{n}}_{\pm}
and
Y_{\mathfrak{l}}\in{\mathfrak{l}}
.

For
2\rho\equiv 2\rho({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})=\mathrm{Trace}(\mathrm{ad}|_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}})\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}
,
we denote by
\mathbb{C}_{2\rho}
the one-dimensional representation of
P

defined by

p\mapsto\chi_{2\rho}(p)=\left|\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{Ad}(p)\colon\mathfrak{n}_{+}\to\mathfrak{n}_{+})\right|
.
For the contragredient representation

((\sigma_{\lambda})^{\vee},V^{\vee})
of
(\sigma_{\lambda},V)
,
we put

\sigma^{*}_{\lambda}:=\sigma^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{2\rho-\lambda}
.
As for
\sigma_{\lambda}
, we regard
\sigma^{*}_{\lambda}
as a representation of
P
.
Define the induced representation

\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}=\mathrm{Ind}_{P}^{G}(\sigma^{*}_{\lambda})

on the space
C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V}^{*})
of smooth sections
for the vector bundle
\mathcal{V}^{*}=G\times_{P}(V^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{2\rho})

associated with
\sigma^{*}_{\lambda}
, which is isomorphic to the tensor bundle
of the dual vector bundle
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}=G\times_{P}V^{\vee}
and the bundle
of densities over
G/P
.
Then the integration on
G/P
gives a

G
-invariant non-degenerate bilinear form

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{P}(\sigma_{\lambda})\times\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{P}(\sigma^{*}_{\lambda})\to\mathbb{C}

for
\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{P}(\sigma_{\lambda})
and

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{P}(\sigma^{*}_{\lambda})
.

As for
C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V})
,
the space
C^{\infty}(G/P,\mathcal{V}^{*})
can be regarded as
a subspace of
C^{\infty}(N_{-})\otimes V^{\vee}
.
Then the infinitesimal representation
d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}(X)

on
C^{\infty}(N_{-})\otimes V^{\vee}

for
X\in{\mathfrak{g}}
is given by

d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}(X)f(\bar{n})=d\sigma_{\lambda}^{*}((\mathrm{Ad}(\bar{n}^{-1})X)_{\mathfrak{l}})f(\bar{n})-\left(dR((\mathrm{Ad}(\cdot^{-1})X)_{{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}})f\right)(\bar{n}).

(2.9)

(For the details, see, for instance,
[
20
, Sect. 2]
.)
Via the exponential map
\exp\colon{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R})\simeq N_{-}
, one can regard

d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}(X)
as a representation
on
C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{n}_{-}(\mathbb{R}))\otimes V^{\vee}
.
It then follows from (
2.9
) that

d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}
gives a Lie algebra homomorphism

d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R});x,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x}]\otimes\mathrm{End}(V^{\vee}),

where
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
are coordinates of
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R})
with
n=\dim{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R})
.
We extend the coordinate functions
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
for
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R})

holomorphically to the ones
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
for
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}
.
Thus we have

d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{-};z,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}]\otimes\mathrm{End}(V^{\vee}).

Now we fix a non-degenerate
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariant symmetric bilinear form
\kappa
on

{\mathfrak{g}}
. Via
\kappa
, we identify

{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}
with the dual space
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}^{\vee}
of

{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}
. Then
the algebraic Fourier transform
\widehat{\;\;\cdot\;\;}

of Weyl algebras (
2.8
) gives a Weyl algebra isomorphism

\widehat{\;\;\cdot\;\;}\;\colon\mathbb{C}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{-};z,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}]\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathbb{C}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{+};\zeta,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\zeta}].

In particular, it gives a Lie algebra homomorphism

\widehat{d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{+};\zeta,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\zeta}]\otimes\mathrm{End}(V^{\vee}).

(2.10)

Now we define a map

F_{c}\colon M_{\mathfrak{p}}(V^{\vee})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})\otimes V^{\vee},\quad u\otimes v^{\vee}\longmapsto\widehat{d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}}(u)(1\otimes v^{\vee}).

(2.11)

Theorem 2.12

(
[
15
, Sect. 3.4]
)
.

The map
F_{c}
is a
(\mathfrak{g},P)
-module isomorphism.

We call
the
(\mathfrak{g},P)
-module isomorphism
F_{c}
in (
2.11
)
the
algebraic Fourier transform of the generalized Verma module
M_{\mathfrak{p}}(V^{\vee})
.

2.5.
The F-method

Observe that
the algebraic Fourier transform

F_{c}
in (
2.11
)
gives an
M^{\prime}A^{\prime}
-representation isomorphism

M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee})^{{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}(\mathrm{Pol}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})\otimes V^{\vee})^{\widehat{d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime})},

(2.13)

which induces a linear isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{M^{\prime}A^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee})^{{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\operatorname{Hom}_{M^{\prime}A^{\prime}}\left(W^{\vee},(\mathrm{Pol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})\otimes V^{\vee})^{\widehat{d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime})}\right).

(2.14)

Here
M^{\prime}A^{\prime}
acts on
\mathrm{Pol}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})
via the action

\mathrm{Ad}_{\#}(l)\colon p(X)\mapsto p(\mathrm{Ad}(l^{-1})X)\;\;\text{for $l\in M^{\prime}A^{\prime}$}.

(2.15)

Now we set

\mathrm{Sol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+};V,W):=\operatorname{Hom}_{M^{\prime}A^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},(\mathrm{Pol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+})\otimes V^{\vee})^{\widehat{d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime})}).

(2.16)

Via the identification

\textrm{Hom}_{M^{\prime}A^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},\mathrm{Pol}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})\otimes V^{\vee})\simeq\big((\mathrm{Pol}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})\otimes V^{\vee})\otimes W\big)^{M^{\prime}A^{\prime}}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sol}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+};V,W)

\displaystyle=\{\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{M^{\prime}A^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},\mathrm{Pol}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})\otimes V^{\vee}):\text{
$\psi$ satisfies the system \eqref{eqn:Fsys} of PDEs below.}\}

(2.17)

(\widehat{d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}}(C)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W})\psi=0\,\,\text{for all $C\in{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}$}.

(2.18)

We refer to the system (
2.18
) of PDEs
as the
F-system
(
[
14
, Fact 3.3 (3)]
).
Since

\operatorname{Hom}_{P^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{M^{\prime}A^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee})^{{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}}),

the isomorphism (
2.14
) together with (
2.16
) shows the following.

Theorem 2.19

(F-method,
[
15
, Thm. 4.1]
)
.

There exists a linear isomorphism

F_{c}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{P^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee}))\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathrm{Sol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+};V,W).

Equivalently, we have

F_{c}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(W^{\vee}),M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee}))\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathrm{Sol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+};V,W).

2.6.
The case of abelian nilradical
{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}

Now suppose that the nilpotent radical
{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}
is abelian.
In this case differential symmetry breaking operators

\mathbb{D}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
have constant coefficients.
(See, for instance,
[
20
, Sect. 2]
.)
Thus, as in (
2.2
), one may view
\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
as

\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})\subset\mathbb{C}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{-};\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}]\otimes\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(V,W).

Since
{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}
is regarded as the dual space of
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}
,
one can define the symbol map

\mathrm{symb}\colon\mathbb{C}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{-};\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[{\mathfrak{n}}_{+};\zeta],\quad\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\mapsto\zeta_{i}.

Theorem
2.20
below shows a beautiful relationship
between the F-method and the symbol map.

Theorem 2.20

(
[
15
, Cor. 4.3]
)
.

Suppose that the nilpotent radical

\mathfrak{n}_{+}
is abelian. Then
the symbol map
\mathrm{symb}
gives
a linear isomorphism

\mathrm{Rest}\circ\mathrm{symb}^{-1}\colon\mathrm{Sol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+};V,W)\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\operatorname{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}).

Further, the diagram (
2.21
) below commutes:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Sol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+};V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\;\;\mathrm{Rest}\,\circ\,\mathrm{symb}^{-1}}
\textstyle{\circlearrowleft{}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(W^{\vee}),M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 25.0pt\sim}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 25.0pt\mathcal{D}_{H\to D}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{F_{c}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}),}

(2.21)

where
\mathrm{Rest}
denotes the restriction map
from
C^{\infty}(G/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})
to
C^{\infty}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime},\mathcal{W})
.

2.7.
A recipe of the F-method for abelian nilradical
{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}

By (
2.17
) and Theorem
2.20
,
one can classify and construct

\mathbb{D}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
and

\Phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(W^{\vee}),M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee}))

by computing
\psi\in\mathrm{Sol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+};V,W)
as follows.

Step 1

Compute
d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}(C)
and

\widehat{d\pi_{(\sigma,\lambda)^{*}}}(C)

for
C\in{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}
.

Step 2

Classify and construct

\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{M^{\prime}A^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},\mathrm{Pol}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})\otimes V^{\vee})
.

Step 3

Solve the F-system (
2.18
)
for
\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{M^{\prime}A^{\prime}}(W^{\vee},\mathrm{Pol}({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})\otimes V^{\vee})
.

Step 4

For
\psi\in\mathrm{Sol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+};V,W)
obtained in Step 3,
do the following.

Step 4a

Apply
\mathrm{Rest}\circ\mathrm{symb}^{-1}

to
\psi\in\mathrm{Sol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+};V,W)

to obtain
\mathbb{D}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
.

Step 4b

Apply
F_{c}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W}

to
\psi\in\mathrm{Sol}(\mathfrak{n}_{+};V,W)

to obtain
\Phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(W^{\vee}),M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(V^{\vee}))
.

3.
Specialization to
(SL(n+1,\mathbb{R}),SL(n,\mathbb{R});P,P^{\prime})

In this section we specialize the general framework
described in Section
2

to the case
(G,G^{\prime})=(SL(n+1,\mathbb{R}),SL(n,\mathbb{R}))
with real flag varieties

G/P\simeq\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
.
Throughout this section we assume
n\geq 2
, unless otherwise specified.

3.1.
Notation

Let
G=SL(n+1,\mathbb{R})
with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathfrak{sl}(n+1,\mathbb{R})
for
n\geq 2
.
Let
G^{\prime}
denote the closed subgroup of
G
such that

G^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}g^{\prime}&\\
&1\\
\end{pmatrix}:g^{\prime}\in SL(n,\mathbb{R})\right\}\simeq SL(n,\mathbb{R})

with Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}X^{\prime}&\\
&0\end{pmatrix}:X^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{R})\right\}\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{R}).

(3.1)

We put

N_{j}^{+}:=E_{1,j+1},\quad N_{j}^{-}:=E_{j+1,1}\quad\text{for $j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$}

and

\displaystyle H_{0}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n+1}(nE_{1,1}-\sum^{n+1}_{r=2}E_{r,r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n+1}\mathrm{diag}(n,-1,-1,\ldots,-1,-1),

(3.2)

\displaystyle H_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n}((n-1)E_{1,1}-\sum^{n}_{r=2}E_{r,r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{diag}(n-1,-1,-1,\ldots,-1,0),

(3.3)

where
E_{i,j}
denote the matrix units. We normalize
H_{0}
and
H_{0}^{\prime}
as

\widetilde{H}_{0}:=\frac{n+1}{n}H_{0}
and
\widetilde{H}_{0}^{\prime}:=\frac{n}{n-1}H_{0}^{\prime}
,
namely,

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}(nE_{1,1}-\sum^{n+1}_{r=2}E_{r,r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{diag}(n,-1,-1,\ldots,-1,-1),

(3.4)

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n-1}((n-1)E_{1,1}-\sum^{n}_{r=2}E_{r,r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n-1}\mathrm{diag}(n-1,-1,-1,\ldots,-1,0).

(3.5)

Let

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{n}}_{\pm}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{ad}(H_{0})\mp\mathrm{id})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{ad}(\widetilde{H}_{0})\mp\tfrac{n+1}{n}\mathrm{id}),

(3.6)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{n}}_{\pm}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{ad}(H_{0}^{\prime})|_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})}\mp\mathrm{id})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{ad}(\widetilde{H}_{0}^{\prime})|_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})}\mp\tfrac{n}{n-1}\mathrm{id}).

Then we have

{\mathfrak{n}}_{\pm}(\mathbb{R})=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{N_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,N_{n-1}^{\pm},N_{n}^{\pm}\}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathfrak{n}}_{\pm}(\mathbb{R})=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{N_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,N_{n-1}^{\pm}\}.

For
X,Y\in{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})
, let
\text{Tr}(X,Y)=\text{Trace}(XY)
denote the trace form of
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then
N_{i}^{+}
and
N_{j}^{-}
satisfy
\text{Tr}(N_{i}^{+},N_{j}^{-})=\delta_{i,j}
.
In what follows, we identify the duals
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R})^{\vee}
of
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R})

and
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})^{\vee}
of
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})

with
{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R})^{\vee}\simeq{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
and

{\mathfrak{n}}_{-}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})^{\vee}\simeq{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})

via the trace form
\text{Tr}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}\widetilde{H}_{0}
and
{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}\widetilde{H}_{0}^{\prime}
.
We also put

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0&\\
&X\end{pmatrix}:X\in\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{R})\right\}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0&&\\
&X^{\prime}&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix}:X^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{sl}(n-1,\mathbb{R})\right\}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(n-1,\mathbb{R}).

Here
\mathfrak{sl}(1,\mathbb{R})
is regarded as
\mathfrak{sl}(1,\mathbb{R})=\{0\}
.
We remark that although
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\simeq{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{R})
, these are
different subalgebras of
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})
.

We have

{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{ad}(\widetilde{H}_{0}))

and

{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{ad}(\widetilde{H}_{0}^{\prime})|_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})})
.
The decompositions

{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})={\mathfrak{n}}_{-}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}(\mathbb{R})

and

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})={\mathfrak{n}}_{-}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})

are Gelfand–Naimark decompositions of
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, resepectively.
The subalgebras

{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{R}):={\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
and

{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}):={\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})

are maximal parabolic
subalgebras of
{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbb{R})
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, respectively.
It is remarked that
{\mathfrak{n}}_{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
and
{\mathfrak{n}}_{\pm}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
are abelian.

Let
P
be the normalizer
N_{G}({\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{R}))
of
{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{R})
in
G

and
P^{\prime}
the normalizer
N_{G^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
of
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
in
G^{\prime}
.
We write
P=MAN_{+}
and
P^{\prime}=M^{\prime}A^{\prime}N_{+}^{\prime}

for the Langlands decompositions of
P
and
P^{\prime}
corresponding to

{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathbb{R})={\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
and

{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})={\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
,
respectively.
Then we have

\displaystyle A=\exp({\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle=\exp(\mathbb{R}\widetilde{H}_{0}),\qquad

\displaystyle N_{+}=\exp({\mathfrak{n}}_{+}(\mathbb{R})),

\displaystyle A^{\prime}=\exp({\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle=\exp(\mathbb{R}\widetilde{H}_{0}^{\prime}),\qquad

\displaystyle N_{+}^{\prime}=\exp({\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})).

The groups
M
and
M^{\prime}
are given by

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\det(g)^{-1}&\\
&g\\
\end{pmatrix}:g\in SL^{\pm}(n,\mathbb{R})\right\}

\displaystyle\simeq SL^{\pm}(n,\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle M^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\det(g^{\prime})^{-1}&&\\
&g^{\prime}&\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}:g^{\prime}\in SL^{\pm}(n-1,\mathbb{R})\right\}

\displaystyle\simeq SL^{\pm}(n-1,\mathbb{R}).

Here
SL^{\pm}(1,\mathbb{R})
is regarded as

SL^{\pm}(1,\mathbb{R})=\{\pm 1\}
.
As
M
and
M^{\prime}
are not connected,
let
M_{0}
and
M_{0}^{\prime}
denote the identity components of
M
and
M^{\prime}
,
respectively.
Then
M_{0}\simeq SL(n,\mathbb{R})
and
M_{0}^{\prime}\simeq SL(n-1,\mathbb{R})
.
For

\gamma=\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,\ldots,1,-1,1)\in M^{\prime}\subset M,

we have

M/M_{0}=\{[I_{n+1}],[\gamma]\}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{and}\quad M^{\prime}/M_{0}^{\prime}=\{[I_{n+1}]^{\prime},[\gamma]^{\prime}\}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},

where
I_{n+1}
denotes the
(n+1)\times(n+1)
identity matrix and

[g]=gM_{0}
and
[g]^{\prime}=gM^{\prime}_{0}
.

Remark 3.7
.

We have

P^{\prime}\subset P
,
M^{\prime}A^{\prime}\subset MA
,
M^{\prime}\subset M
,
N_{\pm}^{\prime}\subset N_{\pm}
,
but
A^{\prime}\not\subset A
. Indeed, each element

a^{\prime}=\mathrm{diag}(t,t^{\frac{-1}{n-1}},\ldots,t^{\frac{-1}{n-1}},1)\in A^{\prime}

can be decomposed as

a^{\prime}=a^{\prime}_{M}a^{\prime}_{A}
with
a^{\prime}_{M}\in M
and
a^{\prime}_{A}\in A
, where

\displaystyle a^{\prime}_{M}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}(1,t^{\frac{-1}{n(n-1)}},\ldots,t^{\frac{-1}{n(n-1)}},t^{\frac{1}{n}})\in M,

\displaystyle a^{\prime}_{A}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{diag}(t,t^{\frac{-1}{n}},\ldots,t^{\frac{-1}{n}},t^{\frac{-1}{n}})\in A.

For a closed subgroup
J
of
G
, we denote by
\mathrm{Irr}(J)
and
\mathrm{Irr}(J)_{\mathrm{fin}}

the sets of equivalence classes of irreducible representations of
J
and
finite-dimensional irreducible representations of
J
, respectively.

For
\lambda,\nu\in\mathbb{C}
,
we define one-dimensional representations

\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}=(\chi^{\lambda},\mathbb{C})
of
A=\exp(\mathbb{R}\widetilde{H}_{0})

and

\mathbb{C}_{\nu}=((\chi^{\prime})^{\nu},\mathbb{C})
of
A^{\prime}=\exp(\mathbb{R}\widetilde{H}_{0}^{\prime})
by

\chi^{\lambda}\colon\exp(t\widetilde{H}_{0})\longmapsto\exp(\lambda t)\quad\text{and}\quad(\chi^{\prime})^{\nu}\colon\exp(t\widetilde{H}_{0}^{\prime})\longmapsto\exp(\nu t).

(3.8)

Then
\mathrm{Irr}(A)
and
\mathrm{Irr}(A^{\prime})
are given by

\mathrm{Irr}(A)=\{\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\}\simeq\mathbb{C}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Irr}(A^{\prime})=\{\mathbb{C}_{\nu}:\nu\in\mathbb{C}\}\simeq\mathbb{C}.

Let
\mathrm{sgn}
denote the sign character of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
.
For
\alpha\in\{\pm\}
, we then define a one-dimensional representation

(\mathrm{sgn}^{\alpha},\mathbb{C})
of
M
by

\begin{pmatrix}\det(g)^{-1}&\\
&g\\
\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto\mathrm{sgn}^{\alpha}(\det(g)).

(3.9)

where

\mathrm{sgn}^{\alpha}(\det(g)):=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\alpha=+$},\\
\mathrm{sgn}(\det(g))&\text{if $\alpha=-$}.\end{cases}

(3.10)

Then

\mathrm{Irr}(M)_{\mathrm{fin}}=\mathrm{Irr}(SL^{\pm}(n,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}

and

\mathrm{Irr}(M^{\prime})_{\mathrm{fin}}=\mathrm{Irr}(SL^{\pm}(n-1,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}

are given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Irr}(M)_{\mathrm{fin}}

\displaystyle\simeq\{\mathrm{sgn}^{\alpha}\otimes\xi:(\alpha,\xi)\in\{\pm\}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Irr}(M^{\prime})_{\mathrm{fin}}

\displaystyle\simeq\{\mathrm{sgn}^{\beta}\otimes\varpi:(\beta,\varpi)\in\{\pm\}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n-1,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\}.

Since

\mathrm{Irr}(P)_{\mathrm{fin}}\simeq\mathrm{Irr}(M)_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathrm{Irr}(A)
, the set
\mathrm{Irr}(P)_{\mathrm{fin}}
can be
parametrized as

\mathrm{Irr}(P)_{\mathrm{fin}}\simeq\{\pm\}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}.

Likewise, we have

\mathrm{Irr}(P^{\prime})_{\mathrm{fin}}\simeq\{\pm\}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n-1,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}.

In particular,
for
n=2
, we have

\mathrm{Irr}(P^{\prime})_{\mathrm{fin}}\simeq\{\pm\}\times\{\mathrm{triv}\}\times\mathbb{C}.

(3.11)

Remark 3.12
.

One needs to be careful
that the parametrization of
\mathrm{Irr}(SL^{\pm}(1,\mathbb{R}))
is not unique.
Indeed, for
m\geq 1
,
we define
\gamma_{m}\in SL^{\pm}(m,\mathbb{R})
as

\gamma_{m}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,-1)&\text{for $m\geq 2$},\\
-1&\text{for $m=1$},\end{cases}

so that
SL^{\pm}(m,\mathbb{R})=\langle\gamma_{m}\rangle\ltimes SL(m,\mathbb{R})
.
Let
\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{m}
denote the irreducible representation of
SL(m,\mathbb{R})
on

S^{k}(\mathbb{C}^{m})
. Here, we regard
\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{1}
with
\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{1}=\mathrm{triv}

as the irreducible representation of
SL(1,\mathbb{R})=\{1\}
on
\mathbb{C}

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Now consider
\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta}\otimes\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{m}\in\mathrm{Irr}(SL^{\pm}(m,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}
for

m\geq 1
.
The irreducible
SL^{\pm}(m,\mathbb{R})
-representation

\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta}\otimes\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{m}
is defined as

(\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta}\otimes\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{m})(g)e_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots e_{m}^{k_{m}}=\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta}(g)(ge_{1})^{k_{1}}\cdots(ge_{m})^{k_{m}},

where
e_{j}
are the standard basis elements of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.
For
m=1
, we then have

\displaystyle(\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta}\otimes\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{1})(\pm 1)1^{k}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sgn}(\pm 1)^{\delta}(\pm 1\cdot 1)^{k}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sgn}(\pm 1)^{\delta}(\pm 1)^{k}1^{k}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sgn}(\pm 1)^{\delta+k}1^{k}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta+k}\otimes\mathrm{triv})(\pm 1)1^{k}.

Here,
for
\delta\in\{\pm\}\equiv\{\pm 1\}

and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we mean

\delta+k\in\{\pm\}
by

\delta+k=\begin{cases}+&\text{if $\delta=(-1)^{k}$},\\
-&\text{if $\delta=(-1)^{k+1}$}.\end{cases}

Therefore, we have

\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta+k}\otimes\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{1}=\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta}\otimes\mathrm{triv}.

(3.13)

Likewise, let
\mathrm{poly}^{k}_{m}
denote the irreducible representation
of
SL(m,\mathbb{R})
on the space
\text{Pol}^{k}(\mathbb{C}^{m})

of polynomial functions on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
of homogeneous degree
k
.
As for
\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{m}
, we regard
\mathrm{poly}^{k}_{1}

as
\mathrm{poly}^{k}_{1}=\mathrm{triv}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Since
\mathrm{poly}^{k}_{m}\simeq(\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{m})^{\vee}
,
the identity (
3.13
) implies that

\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta+k}\otimes\mathrm{poly}^{k}_{1}=\mathrm{sgn}^{\delta}\otimes\mathrm{triv}.

(3.14)

For

(\alpha,\xi,\lambda)\in\{\pm\}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}

and

(\beta,\varpi,\nu)\in\{\pm\}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n-1,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}
,
we write

I(\xi,\lambda)^{\alpha}=\mathrm{Ind}_{P}^{G}\left((\mathrm{sgn}^{\alpha}\otimes\xi)\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad J(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta}=\mathrm{Ind}_{P^{\prime}}^{G^{\prime}}\left((\mathrm{sgn}^{\beta}\otimes\varpi)\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{\nu}\right)

(3.15)

for (unnormalized) parabolically induced representations
of
G
and of
G^{\prime}
, respectively.
For instance, the unitary axis of

J(\mathrm{triv},\nu)^{\beta}
is
\text{Re}(\nu)=\frac{n}{2}
,
where
\mathrm{triv}
denotes the trivial representation of
SL(n-1,\mathbb{R})
.
(See, for instance,
[
26
, p. 102]
and
[
8
, p. 298]
.
We remark that the complex parameters “
\mu
” in
[
26
]
,
“
\alpha
” in
[
8
]
, and “
\nu
” in this paper are related as

\mu=\alpha=-\nu
.)

For the representation spaces
V
and
W

of

(\alpha,\xi,\lambda)\in\mathrm{Irr}(P)_{\mathrm{fin}}

and of
(\beta,\varpi,\nu)\in\mathrm{Irr}(P^{\prime})_{\mathrm{fin}}
, respectively,
we write

M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\xi,\lambda)^{\alpha}=\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{p}})}V\quad\text{and}\quad M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta}=\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime})}W.

(3.16)

In the next section we classify and construct
differential symmetry breaking operators

\mathbb{D}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta})

and
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-homomorphisms

\Phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\varpi,\nu)^{\alpha},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\beta}).

If
n=2
, then (
3.14
) and (
3.13
) show that,
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle J(\mathrm{triv},\nu)^{\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ind}_{P^{\prime}}^{SL(2,\mathbb{R})}\left((\mathrm{sgn}^{\beta}\otimes\mathrm{triv})\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ind}_{P^{\prime}}^{SL(2,\mathbb{R})}((\mathrm{sgn}^{\beta+k}\otimes\mathrm{poly}^{k}_{1})\boxtimes\mathbb{C}_{\nu})

\displaystyle=J(\mathrm{poly}^{k}_{1},\nu)^{\beta+k}

(3.17)

and

M^{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{triv},\nu)^{\beta}=M^{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{1},\nu)^{\beta+k}.

(3.18)

4.
Differential symmetry breaking operators
\mathbb{D}
and
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-homomorphisms
\Phi

The objective of this section is to state the main results of
the classification and
construction of
differential symmetry breaking operators

\mathbb{D}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta})
as well as

({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-homomorphism

\Phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\varpi,\nu)^{\alpha},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\beta})
.
These are achieved in Theorems

4.15
and

4.16

for
\mathbb{D}
and Theorems

4.23

and

4.24

for
\Phi
.
In addition, we also present the classification of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
-homomorphisms between
generalized Verma modules in Section
4.3
.

The proofs will be discussed in detail in Sections
5
and

6
in accordance with the recipe of the F-method.
In this section we assume
n\geq 2
, unless otherwise
specified.

4.1.
Differential symmetry breaking operators
\mathbb{D}

We start with the classification and construction results of
differential symmetry breaking operators
\mathbb{D}
.
In this subsection,
let
(\alpha,\beta;\varpi;\lambda,\nu)\in\{\pm\}^{2}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n-1,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
parametrize a pair
(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta})
of induced representations
of
G
and
G^{\prime}
, respectively.
If
n=2
, then, via the identity (
3.17
), we regard

(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv};\lambda,\nu)
as

(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv};\lambda,\nu)=(\alpha,\beta+k;\mathrm{poly}^{k}_{1};\lambda,\nu)

(4.1)

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
We then define subsets

\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL,j}\subset\{\pm\}^{2}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n-1,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}

for
j=1,2
as

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL,1}

\displaystyle:=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m;\mathrm{triv};\lambda,\lambda+m):\alpha\in\{\pm\},\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\;\text{and}\;m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL,2}

\displaystyle:=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m+\ell;\mathrm{poly}_{n-1}^{\ell};1-(m+\ell),1+\tfrac{\ell}{n-1}):\alpha\in\{\pm\}\;\text{and}\;\ell,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

(4.3)

If
n=2
, then
\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,j}
for
j=1,2
are given by

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,1}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m;\mathrm{triv};\lambda,\lambda+m):\alpha\in\{\pm\},\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\text{and}\;m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\},

(4.4)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,2}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m+\ell;\mathrm{poly}^{\ell}_{1};1-(m+\ell),1+\ell):\alpha\in\{\pm\}\;\text{and}\;\ell,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}

(4.5)

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m;\mathrm{triv};1-(m+\ell),1+\ell):\alpha\in\{\pm\}\;\text{and}\;\ell,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

(4.6)

For
n=2
, we further put

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,+}

\displaystyle:=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m+\ell;\mathrm{poly}^{\ell}_{1};1-(m+\ell),1+\ell):\alpha\in\{\pm\},m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\;\text{and}\;\ell\in 1+\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}

(4.7)

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m;\mathrm{triv};1-(m+\ell),1+\ell):\alpha\in\{\pm\},m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\;\text{and}\;\ell\in 1+\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

(4.8)

We set

\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL}:=\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL,1}\cup\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL,2}.

(4.9)

As

\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,+}\subset\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,2}\subset\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,1},

we have

\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL}=\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,1}.

We consider the cases
n\geq 3
and
n=2
, separately.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
n\geq 3
.
The following three conditions on

(\alpha,\beta;\varpi;\lambda,\nu)\in\{\pm\}^{2}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n-1,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
are
equivalent.

(i)

\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}\big(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta})\neq\{0\}
.

(ii)

\dim\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}\big(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta})=1
.

(iii)

(\alpha,\beta;\varpi;\lambda,\nu)\in\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL}
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
n=2
.
The following three conditions on

(\alpha,\beta;\lambda,\nu)\in\{\pm\}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
are
equivalent.

(i)

\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}\big(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\mathrm{triv},\nu)^{\beta}\big)\neq\{0\}
.

(ii)

\dim\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}\big(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\mathrm{triv},\nu)^{\beta}\big)\in\{1,2\}
.

(iii)

(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv};\lambda,\nu)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,1}
.

The dimension is two if and only if

(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv};\lambda,\nu)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,+}
.

We next consider the explicit formula of

\mathbb{D}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta})
.
We write

\mathrm{Pol}^{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n-1})=\mathbb{C}^{k}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}].

In what follows, we identify
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
as a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with

\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\simeq\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},0):x_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\}.

(4.12)

Then,
as in (
2.2
),
we understand

\mathbb{D}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta})

for
(\alpha,\beta;\varpi;\lambda,\nu)\in\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL}

as a map

\mathbb{D}\colon C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{k}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}]

via the diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}N_{-},\quad(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},x_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(x_{1}N^{-}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1}N^{-}_{n-1}+x_{n}N^{-}_{n}),

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}N_{-}^{\prime},\quad(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},0)

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(x_{1}N^{-}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1}N^{-}_{n-1}).

For
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we put

\Xi_{\ell}^{\prime}:=\{(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n-1})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{n-1}:\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\ell_{j}=\ell\}.

For
\textbf{l}=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n-1})\in\Xi_{\ell}^{\prime}
, we write

\displaystyle y_{\mathbf{l}}

\displaystyle=y_{1}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots y_{n-1}^{\ell_{n-1}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}_{\mathbf{l}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\ell_{1}!\cdots\ell_{n-1}!}\cdot y_{\mathbf{l}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{\ell}}{\partial x^{\mathbf{l}}}\

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{\ell}}{\partial x_{1}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{n-1}^{\ell_{n-1}}}.

Let

\mathrm{Rest}_{x_{n}=0}\colon C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
be the restriction operator
from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
via the inclusion
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
in
(
4.12
). Namely, for
f(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with

x^{\prime}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})
, we have

(\mathrm{Rest}_{x_{n}=0}f)(x^{\prime})=f(x^{\prime},0).

For
m,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
we define

\mathbb{D}_{(m,\ell)}\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\mathbb{C}}(C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m+\ell}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}])
by

\mathbb{D}_{(m,\ell)}:=\mathrm{Rest}_{x_{n}=0}\circ\frac{\partial^{m}}{\partial x_{n}^{m}}\sum_{\mathbf{l}\in\Xi_{\ell}^{\prime}}\frac{\partial^{\ell}}{\partial x^{\mathbf{l}}}\otimes\widetilde{y}_{\mathbf{l}}.

(4.14)

In particular, we have

\mathbb{D}_{(m,0)}=\mathrm{Rest}_{x_{n}=0}\circ\frac{\partial^{m}}{\partial x_{n}^{m}}.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
n\geq 3
. Then we have

\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}\big(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\varpi,\nu)^{\beta}\big)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{D}_{(m,0)}&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta;\varpi;\lambda,\nu)\in\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL,1}$,}\\[3.0pt]
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{D}_{(m,\ell)}&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta;\varpi;\lambda,\nu)\in\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{SL,2}$,}\\
\{0\}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
n=2
. Then we have

\mathrm{Diff}_{G^{\prime}}\big(I(\mathrm{triv},\lambda)^{\alpha},J(\mathrm{triv},\nu)^{\beta}\big)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{D}_{(m,0)}&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv}\lambda,\nu)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,1}\backslash\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,+}$,}\\[3.0pt]
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{D}_{(m+2\ell,0)}\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathbb{D}_{(m,\ell)}&\text{if $(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv};\lambda,\nu)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{SL,+}$,}\\
\{0\}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

4.2.
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-homomorphisms

We next consider
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
-homomorphisms
\Phi
.
As for Section
4.1
,

(\alpha,\beta;\sigma;s,r)\in\{\pm\}^{2}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n-1,\mathbb{C}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
indicates
a pair
(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\sigma,r)^{\beta},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}\big)
of generalized Verma modules
of
({\mathfrak{g}},P)
and
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})
, respectively.
If
n=2
, then, via the identity (
3.18
), we regard

(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv};s,r)
as

(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv};s,r)=(\alpha,\beta+k;\mathrm{sym}^{k}_{1};s,r)

(4.17)

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

For
n\geq 2
,
define
\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),j}\subset\{\pm\}^{2}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n-1,\mathbb{R}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
for
j=1,2
as

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),1}

\displaystyle:=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m;\mathrm{triv};s,s-m):\alpha\in\{\pm\},s\in\mathbb{C},\;\text{and}\;m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\},

(4.18)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),2}

\displaystyle:=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m+\ell;\mathrm{sym}_{n-1}^{\ell};(m+\ell)-1,-(1+\tfrac{\ell}{n-1})):\alpha\in\{\pm\}\;\text{and}\;\ell,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

(4.19)

For
n=2
, the sets
\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),j}
for
j=1,2
are given by

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),1}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m;\mathrm{triv};s,s-m):\alpha\in\{\pm\},s\in\mathbb{C}\;\text{and}\;m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\},

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),2}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m+\ell;\mathrm{sym}^{\ell}_{1};(m+\ell)-1,-(1+\ell)):\alpha\in\{\pm\}\;\text{and}\;\ell,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m;\mathrm{triv};(m+\ell)-1,-(1+\ell)):\alpha\in\{\pm\}\;\text{and}\;\ell,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

Further, we put

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),+}

\displaystyle:=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m+\ell;\mathrm{sym}^{\ell}_{1};(m+\ell)-1,-(1+\ell)):\alpha\in\{\pm\},m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\;\text{and}\;\ell\in 1+\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\alpha+m;\mathrm{triv};(m+\ell)-1,-(1+\ell)):\alpha\in\{\pm\},m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\;\text{and}\;\ell\in 1+\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

We set

\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})}:=\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),1}\cup\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),2}.

Since

\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),+}\subset\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),2}\subset\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),1},

we have

\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})}=\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),1}.

As in Section
4.1
, we consider the cases
n\geq 3
and
n=2
,
separately.

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
n\geq 3
. The following three conditions on

(\alpha,\beta;\sigma;s,r)\in\{\pm\}^{2}\times\mathrm{Irr}(SL(n-1,\mathbb{C}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}

are equivalent.

(i)

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\big(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\sigma,r)^{\beta},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}\big)\neq\{0\}
.

(ii)

\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\big(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\sigma,r)^{\beta},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}\big)=1
.

(iii)

(\alpha,\beta;\sigma;s,r)\in\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime})}
.

Theorem 4.21
.

Let
n=2
. The following three conditions on

(\alpha,\beta;s,r)\in\{\pm\}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}

are equivalent.

(i)

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\big(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{triv},r)^{\beta},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}\big)\neq\{0\}
.

(ii)

\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\big(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{triv},r)^{\beta},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}\big)\in\{1,2\}
.

(iii)

(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv};s,r)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),1}
.

The dimension is two if and only if

(\alpha,\beta;\mathrm{triv};s,r)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),+}
.

To give the explicit formulas of

\Phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\sigma,r)^{\beta},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha})
,
we write

S^{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n-1})=\mathbb{C}^{k}[e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1}],

where
e_{j}
are the standard basis elements of
\mathbb{C}^{n-1}
.

For
\mathbf{l}=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n-1})\in\Xi_{\ell}^{\prime}
, we write

\displaystyle e_{\mathbf{l}}

\displaystyle=e_{1}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots e_{n-1}^{\ell_{n-1}}

\displaystyle\in S^{\ell}(\mathbb{C}^{n-1}),

\displaystyle N_{\mathbf{l}}^{-}

\displaystyle=(N_{1}^{-})^{\ell_{1}}\cdots(N_{n-1}^{-})^{\ell_{n-1}}

\displaystyle\in S^{\ell}({\mathfrak{n}}_{-}).

Observe that we have

\mathbb{C}^{\ell}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1}]=\mathrm{Pol}^{\ell}(\mathbb{C}^{n-1})=S^{\ell}((\mathbb{C}^{n-1})^{\vee})\simeq S^{\ell}(\mathbb{C}^{n-1})^{\vee}.

We then define
y_{j}\in(\mathbb{C}^{n-1})^{\vee}
in such a way that
y_{i}(e_{j})=\delta_{i,j}
,
which gives

\widetilde{y}_{\mathbf{l}}(e_{\mathbf{l}^{\prime}})=\delta_{\mathbf{l},\mathbf{l}^{\prime}}

for
\mathbf{l},\mathbf{l}^{\prime}\in\Xi_{\ell}^{\prime}
.

For
m,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
we define
\Phi_{(m,\ell)}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(S^{\ell}(\mathbb{C}^{n-1}),S^{m+\ell}({\mathfrak{n}}_{-}))
by means of

\Phi_{(m,\ell)}:=(N_{n}^{-})^{m}\sum_{\mathbf{l}\in\Xi_{\ell}^{\prime}}N_{\mathbf{l}}^{-}\otimes(e_{\mathbf{l}})^{\vee}=(N_{n}^{-})^{m}\sum_{\mathbf{l}\in\Xi_{\ell}^{\prime}}N_{\mathbf{l}}^{-}\otimes\widetilde{y}_{\mathbf{l}}.

(4.22)

In particular, we have

\Phi_{(m,0)}=(N_{n}^{-})^{m}.

Since
M_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}\simeq S({\mathfrak{n}}_{-})
as linear spaces,
we have

\Phi_{(m,\ell)}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(S^{\ell}(\mathbb{C}^{n-1}),M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}).

Further, the following hold.

Theorem 4.23
.

Let
n\geq 3
. We have

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\big(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\sigma,r)^{\beta},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}\big)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m,0)}\quad&\text{if $(\sigma;s,r)\in\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),1}$},\\[3.0pt]
\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m,\ell)}\quad&\text{if $(\sigma;s,r)\in\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),2}$},\\
\{0\}\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Theorem 4.24
.

For
n=2
, we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\big(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{triv},r)^{\beta},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}\big)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m,0)}\quad&\text{if $(\mathrm{triv};s,r)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),1}\backslash\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),+}$},\\[3.0pt]
\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m+2\ell,0)}\oplus\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m,\ell)}\quad&\text{if $(\mathrm{triv};s,r)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}),+}$},\\
\{0\}\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Here, by abuse of notation, we regard

\Phi_{(m,\ell)}
as a map

\Phi_{(m,\ell)}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\big(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\sigma,r)^{\beta},M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)^{\alpha}\big)

defined by

\Phi_{(m,\ell)}(u\otimes w):=u\Phi_{(m,\ell)}(w)\quad\text{for $u\in\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})$ and $w\in S^{\ell}(\mathbb{C}^{n-1})$.}

(4.25)

4.3.
Classification of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
-homomorphisms

Finally, we consider
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
-homomorphisms.
For
n\geq 2
, we define
\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},j}\subset\mathrm{Irr}(\mathfrak{sl}(n-1,\mathbb{C}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
for
j=1,2
such that

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},1}

\displaystyle:=\{(\mathrm{triv};s,s-m):s\in\mathbb{C}\;\text{and}\;m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\},

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},2}

\displaystyle:=\{(\mathrm{sym}_{n-1}^{\ell};(m+\ell)-1,-(1+\tfrac{\ell}{n-1})):\ell,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

For
n=2
, we further put

\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},+}:=\{(\mathrm{triv};(m+\ell)-1,-(1+\ell)):m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\;\text{and}\;\ell\in 1+\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}.

We have

\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},+}\subset\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},2}\subset\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},1}.

As in (
3.16
),
for
(\eta,s)\in\mathrm{Irr}(\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}
and

(\sigma,r)\in\mathrm{Irr}(\mathfrak{sl}(n-1,\mathbb{C}))_{\mathrm{fin}}\times\mathbb{C}
,
we define generalized Verma modules
M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\eta,s)
and
M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\sigma,r)

as a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
-module, respectively.

Theorem 4.26
.

For
n\geq 3
, we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}\big(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\sigma,r),M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)\big)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m,0)}\quad&\text{if $(\sigma;s,r)\in\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},1}$},\\[3.0pt]
\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m,\ell)}\quad&\text{if $(\sigma;s,r)\in\Lambda^{(n+1,n)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},2}$},\\
\{0\}\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Theorem 4.27
.

For
n=2
, we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}\big(M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{triv},r),M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)\big)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m,0)}\quad&\text{if $(\mathrm{triv};s,r)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},1}\backslash\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},+}$},\\[3.0pt]
\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m+2\ell,0)}\oplus\mathbb{C}\Phi_{(m,\ell)}\quad&\text{if $(\mathrm{triv};s,r)\in\Lambda^{(3,2)}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime},+}$},\\
\{0\}\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Remark 4.28
.

Let
n\geq 2
.
Since the generalized Verma module
M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{triv},s-m)
is of scalar type,
the
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
-homomorphisms

\varphi_{(m,0)}\colon M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{triv},s-m)\to M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)
are all injective
(cf.
[
9
, Prop. 9.11]
). Theorems
4.26
and
4.27
then
imply that we have

\bigoplus_{m=0}^{\infty}M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{triv},s-m)\hookrightarrow M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s).

(4.29)

In Section
9
, we shall show that

M^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\mathrm{triv},s)|_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}

is in fact isomorphic to

\bigoplus_{m=0}^{\infty}M^{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{triv},s-m)

for all
s\in\mathbb{C}
as
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
-modules
(see Theorem
9.22
). Further, we shall also discuss the
multiplicity-two phenomenon for
n=2
from
a viewpoint of branching laws (see Remark
9.37
).

5.
Proofs for the classification and construction of
\mathbb{D}
and
\Phi
:
Case
n\geq 3

In the present and next sections,
we follow the recipe of the F-method in Section
2.7

to prove the theorems in Section
4
.
Since the arguments are slightly different between
the cases
n\geq 3
and
n=2
, we consider the two cases, separately.

In this section we deal with the case
n\geq 3
, that is, we show
Theorems
4.10
,
4.15
,
4.20
,

4.23
, and
4.26
. The case
n=2
is considered separately
in Section
6
.

5.1.
Step 1:
Compute
d\pi_{(\xi,\lambda)^{*}}(C)
and

\widehat{d\pi_{(\xi,\lambda)^{*}}}(C)

for
C\in{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}^{\prime}
.

For
\xi=\alpha\otimes\mathrm{triv}
and
\lambda\equiv\chi^{\lambda}
,
we simply write

d\pi_{\lambda^{*}}=d\pi_{(\alpha\otimes\mathrm{triv},\chi^{\lambda})^{*}}

with
\lambda^{*}=2\rho({\mathfrak{n}}_{+})-\lambda d\chi
.

The operators

d\pi_{(\xi,\lambda)^{*}}(N_{j}^{+})
and

\widehat{d\pi_{(\xi,\lambda)^{*}}}(N_{j}^{+})

are already computed in
[
20
, Sect. 5]
for
N_{j}^{+}\in{\mathfrak{n}}_{+}
.
We thus simply recall those formulas from the cited paper in this subsection.
We remark that the formulas are not only for
